[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=113 session=1f02878a-1847-414e-b657-d91660105c2b]
timestamp: 2026-10-31T08:33:25.737Z
time: 2026-10-31 08:33
chars: 2101

Supadata works with your real key. With `yt-dlp` and `uvx` hidden to imitate Vercel, reading the photosynthesis video took about 5 seconds and returned all 412 words in 30 segments. That's the same word and segment count as the transcript stored for that video on hosted, and Gemini took about 8 seconds on this test.

**Testing found two problems, both fixed:**
- **429 errors:** I was sending both calls at once, so the transcript was refused and the read silently fell back to Gemini. The calls now run one after another, with one short retry.
- **Rough timestamps:** Supadata's default chunks run about 25 seconds, so concept timestamps could be far off. I now ask for smaller chunks, which brings segments down to roughly 10 seconds and timestamps in line with the other read paths.

**Cost:** each read uses 2 credits, one for the transcript and one for the title and length. The free 100 credits a month cover about 50 video reads, assuming your 100 is the full allowance. Testing used about 12 of them. Once a video is read it's saved, so reading it again doesn't use credits. On your local machine it only spends credits when `yt-dlp` fails or is rate-limited.

**Limits to know about:**
- **Not tested on Vercel:** I imitated it locally. Supadata does the YouTube fetching, so it shouldn't matter where the call comes from, but the first real deploy still needs checking. Add `SUPADATA_API_KEY` in Vercel at that point.
- **Quota running out:** if the 100 credits run out, a read should quietly fall back to Gemini, but I haven't seen that happen.
- **Videos with no speech:** on Big Buck Bunny, which has no dialogue, it returned just "Thank you." as the whole transcript. A nearly speechless video could therefore be treated as having captions, and I left that as is.
- **Videos with no captions:** I didn't test one with speech, so Gemini taking over in that case is unconfirmed.

Tests, typecheck, lint and formatting pass on the final code. The other local checks passed just before the last small fix, and the production build still has to run before the PR, as it stops the local API.

The "I'll be back" help panel works in the app. In a real class on hosted Supabase, I tapped it on Omar's question and got this at 1:58:

> When the chlorophyll in the leaves receives sunlight, the process begins by transforming the raw sap into elaborated sap… it leaves out the specific chemical or cellular details…

**How it works:**
- **Private:** only you see it. It isn't saved, and neither student receives it.
- **From the video only:** it explains the question in two or three short sentences using only the transcript, and says what the video leaves out.
- **Real timestamps:** the server takes the time from the transcript, so the AI can't invent one.
- **Speed and cost:** it took under 4 seconds, and each question is only fetched once, so reopening the panel costs nothing.
- **Tracking:** answering after opening it saves the turn as `needed_help`, which I confirmed on hosted. That's the outcome the assessment screen will show, and opening the panel fires a new `class_help_opened` event.

**Fixed while testing:** the first version answered "The video does not cover this." for a question the video mostly does cover, so I loosened that rule. I also told it to say "the video" instead of "the transcript", but that last wording change hasn't been seen in a live answer.

**Known gaps:**
- **Resume:** which questions you used help on is only remembered on the screen. If you leave and come back to a class, that question saves as `answered` unless you reopen help, which would need a small database column to fix.
- **Opening it counts:** tapping the panel marks the turn as needing help even if you close it straight away.
- **Timestamp display:** the wireframe shows the time as a chip, but here it's plain text before "Omar is still waiting".
- **Spelling:** it's labelled "I'll be back" as in the brief, but the button text is the only hint of what it does.

No, I didn't remove it. `yt-dlp` is still in the code and still runs first everywhere, and I only added Supadata as the next step after it. We also never actually used `yt-dlp` on the cloud, because the app has never been deployed. The only time it ran there was in my throwaway test, where YouTube blocked it.

The order is the same on your Mac and on a server:
1. **`yt-dlp`:** on your Mac it normally gets the captions for free, and Supadata isn't called unless it fails or is rate-limited.
2. **Supadata:** on Vercel, `yt-dlp` isn't installed, so that step should fail right away and move here.
3. **Gemini:** handles videos with no captions, or anything Supadata can't get.

So keeping `yt-dlp` shouldn't slow hosted reads or cost anything there. It just saves Supadata credits while you work locally.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=122 session=1f02878a-1847-414e-b657-d91660105c2b]
timestamp: 2026-10-31T09:40:11.950Z
time: 2026-10-31 09:40
chars: 2531

In production, a person uses the phone app, which calls your Next.js API on Vercel, backed by hosted Supabase and Gemini. It isn't deployed yet, so this is how it's built to run, and I've only tested parts of it on Vercel.

**1. Sign in**
The app signs in through Supabase and sends a token with each request, and the server checks that token.

**2. Paste a YouTube link**
- If that person already read this video, the saved copy is used, with no credits or AI cost.
- Otherwise the API tries `yt-dlp`, which isn't installed on Vercel, so that step fails right away.
- **Supadata** then gets the title, length and existing captions, for 2 credits.
- If the video has no captions, or Supadata fails, **Gemini** watches the video and writes the transcript. That's slower and costs AI tokens.
- With captions the limit is 90 minutes, and without them it's 30.
- Gemini then picks the key concepts, keeping only quotes it can find in the transcript, and everything is saved to the database.

**3. Explain the video**
The person types their explanation in their own words.

**4. Teach the class**
- Maya and Omar each raise a hand with a question ready. Maya only knows what the person told her, while Omar has seen the video.
- The person picks who to answer, for up to 4 questions.
- After each answer, Gemini checks whether it helped. That student then follows up or asks something new.
- "I'll be back: check the video" opens the private explanation, and answering after that saves as `needed_help`.
- The person can skip a question, end the class early, or leave and come back to it.

**5. The class ends**
It shows how many questions they answered, and that's the end of the app today. Scoring, gaps, the assessment and warm-ups aren't built yet (8X-15 onward).

**Before this can run in production:**
- **Deploy the API:** create the Vercel project and add all its environment variables, including `SUPADATA_API_KEY`.
- **Ship the app:** the phone app needs a build pointing at that API, and the browser version would need its own hosting.
- **Fix migrations:** `Deploy migrations` is switched off, so future database changes won't apply on their own.
- **Check speed:** class questions took 10–12 seconds from your Mac because of database round trips to Ireland. Running Vercel in the same region as the database should cut that a lot, though I haven't measured it.
- **Watch Supadata credits:** the free plan covers about 50 new video reads a month. After that, reads should fall back to Gemini, but I haven't seen that happen.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=123 session=1f02878a-1847-414e-b657-d91660105c2b]
timestamp: 2026-10-31T09:42:36.989Z
time: 2026-10-31 09:42
chars: 2838

Production now tries Gemini first and falls back to Supadata. On your Mac it still uses `yt-dlp`, then Supadata, then Gemini, because you asked for this order in production only. Here are the two runs in production mode on your Mac:

| Test | What happened | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Gemini working | Gemini read the video, and Supadata wasn't called | 7.5 seconds, 31 segments, 412 words |
| Gemini failing (bad key) | It fell back to Supadata | 9.3 seconds, 30 segments, 412 words |

Supadata also takes over if the YouTube title lookup refuses a video, or if Gemini refuses to transcribe it or finds it too long. If Supadata has no captions either, the person sees the original error.

**The Gemini prompt for reading a video** is sent with the YouTube link attached, at temperature 0, and its reply must be JSON:

```text
Transcribe everything spoken in this video, word for word, from the very start to the very end. Do not summarise or skip anything. Split it into segments of one or two sentences. Give each segment the MM:SS time it starts in the video (H:MM:SS past the first hour). If nobody speaks in the video, return no segments. Never invent speech.
```

**The key concepts prompt** runs next as a system instruction, with the transcript as the message, also at temperature 0:

```text
You read the transcript of an educational video and pick the ideas someone must be able to explain to show they understood it. Pick 5 to 10 key concepts, in the order the video covers them. Use only what the transcript says. Do not add outside knowledge. Skip jokes, asides, greetings, sponsors and requests to subscribe. Give each concept a short title. Give each concept one quote: 8 to 25 consecutive words copied exactly from the transcript, the place where the video states that idea. Never paraphrase, shorten or join separate sentences.
```

**What this order changes:**
- **Long videos cost more:** Gemini now runs before anything knows the video's length. A 2-hour video gets fully transcribed, paid for, and only then rejected as too long. Supadata would then read it if it's under 90 minutes with captions, so you'd pay both.
- **Every new video uses Gemini tokens:** captions were free in tokens, and Gemini is also slower on long videos. Vercel's time limit could stop a long read before it finishes, and that wouldn't trigger the Supadata fallback, because the whole request gets killed.
- **Upside:** Supadata credits are only used when needed, so 100 credits go much further.

A cheap fix for the long-video cost is to get the length first, for example from the YouTube Data API. Tell me if you want that added.

This is uncommitted on the help panel branch alongside that work. The tests pass, but the production build hasn't run yet, and the fallback hasn't been run on Vercel. The run with Supadata used 2 more credits.

Yes, all five AI calls use `gemini-3.5-flash-lite`:

| Call | What it does | Temperature |
|---|---|---|
| `video_transcript` | Reads the video when Gemini is used | 0 |
| `key_concepts` | Picks the 5–10 concepts | 0 |
| `student_turn` | Writes Maya's and Omar's questions | 0.7 |
| `student_follow_up` | Checks whether an answer helped | 0 |
| `class_help` | Writes the private "I'll be back" explanation | 0.2 |

The model name is set separately in four files, with `student_follow_up` sharing the `student_turn` setting. That means you can change one call's model without touching the others.

I'd keep flash-lite for everything except scoring (8X-15) when that's built. It handles the simple calls fine and it's quick, and I've only seen it make small mistakes, like Omar asking two questions in one turn. Scoring decides what someone got right or wrong, and the rest of the app builds on those gaps, so it's the one call where a stronger model could be worth the extra cost. Test both on the same explanations before deciding.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=125 session=1f02878a-1847-414e-b657-d91660105c2b]
timestamp: 2026-10-31T10:12:03.351Z
time: 2026-10-31 10:12
chars: 847

Yes, all five Gemini calls use structured output. Each one sets `responseMimeType: 'application/json'` and a `responseJsonSchema`, so Gemini has to reply in the exact shape we define:

| Call | Shape it must return |
|---|---|
| `video_transcript` | Segments with a time and text |
| `key_concepts` | Concepts with a title and quote |
| `student_turn` | `{ question }` |
| `student_follow_up` | `{ follow_up: true or false }` |
| `class_help` | `{ explanation, segment }` |

The replies are also checked on our side, so a malformed reply counts as a real failure and never becomes partial data. Each of the student and help parsers has a test for that, and transcript and key-concept results get their own safeguards. For example, concept quotes must actually appear in the transcript, and the help timestamp comes from the transcript, not the AI.

Here's a prompt to paste into namemyapp's box. It's written for naming the app, so it covers what makes the app different and what to avoid:

```text
A mobile learning app where you learn by teaching. Paste a YouTube video you watched, explain it in your own words, then teach it to two AI students. Maya is a curious beginner who only knows what you tell her. Omar has seen the video and checks you against it. They raise their hands, ask about what you got wrong or left out, and you pick who to answer. If you get stuck, say "I'll be back", learn it privately from the video, and return to answer. Afterwards you see your gaps, and short warm-ups from students who remember you bring them back over time, so what you learned actually sticks.

Audience: students, self-learners and anyone who watches educational videos but forgets them.

Feeling: warm, smart and a little playful, like a classroom that's on your side, not a strict exam app.

Name ideas should suggest teaching, explaining, a mentor and student, or learning that sticks. The working name is Protégé, so suggest alternatives in that spirit.

Rules: short, easy to say and spell, works worldwide, one or two words. Avoid generic words like "Learn", "Study", "Edu" or "AI" on their own. Prefer an available .com, .app or .ai.
```

The gaps and warm-ups in this prompt aren't built yet, but they're in your brief, so a name should cover the full idea.

If the box only takes a short line, use this:

```text
A mobile app where you learn a YouTube video by teaching it to AI students who ask about what you missed, then remember you and bring your gaps back in warm-ups. Warm, smart, playful. Name should suggest teaching or learning that sticks.
```

Pick the name with Arif before buying a domain, because 8X-31 is still unassigned and the choice affects you both.

My pick is **Chalkback**. It sounds like a real brand, and it holds the app's main idea: you're at the chalkboard, and the class talks back.

**Best fit for the idea:**

| Name | Why it works |
|---|---|
| **Chalkback** | Your students answer back, and it's memorable |
| **Teachback** | "Teach-back" is a real learning method, so the name explains the app, though it sounds more clinical |
| **Handsup** | The raised hands are your signature moment; simple and playful |
| **Showhands** | A twist on "show of hands", with more personality |

**Warm and playful:**

| Name | Why it works |
|---|---|
| **Pupil** | Short and warm, and you're the one with students |
| **Classmate** | Friendly and easy to say, but common |
| **Tinyclass** | Cute, and it fits two small students who remember you |
| **Sensei** | You become the teacher, but it's already widely used |

**About remembering:**

| Name | Why it works |
|---|---|
| **Sticky** | Matches the hackathon theme, "Learning that sticks" |
| **Recallroom** | Clear, but a little long |
| **Stuck** | Bold, since it's where you get stuck and what sticks, but it may sound negative |

I'd compare **Chalkback**, **Handsup** and **Teachback** with Arif. Protégé is a nice word, but it points at the wrong person: in your app, the user is the teacher and the AI students are the protégés. It's also hard to type with the accent.

I haven't checked domains, trademarks or app store names for any of these. Run your favourites through namemyapp first, since common words like Pupil and Sticky are likely taken or already used by other apps.

The Vercel project is set up and connected to GitHub, but it can't deploy yet because it has no environment variables. Copying your secrets into Vercel was blocked by the permission system, and I didn't try to work around that.

**Done:**
- Created project `chalkback` on your Vercel account (`shayankhanx1x`).
- Connected it to `devv-shayan/8x-hackathon`, deploying `main` to production, with previews for branches and PRs.
- Set the root directory to `apps/web` and the framework to Next.js.

**Needs you:** add these in Vercel → `chalkback` → Settings → Environment Variables, for Production and Preview:

| Variable | Value |
|---|---|
| `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL` | Same as `apps/web/.env.local` |
| `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY` | Same as `apps/web/.env.local` |
| `SUPABASE_SECRET_KEY` | Same as `apps/web/.env.local` |
| `GEMINI_API_KEY` | Same as `apps/web/.env.local` |
| `SUPADATA_API_KEY` | Same as `apps/web/.env.local` |
| `DATABASE_URL` | Same as `apps/web/.env.local`, with `&connection_limit=1` added at the end |
| `REVENUECAT_WEBHOOK_SECRET` | New, from the command below. **Production builds fail without it** |
| `CRON_SECRET` | New, from the command below |
| `DASHBOARD_API_KEY` | New, from the command below |
| `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` | `https://chalkback.vercel.app`, or the real domain Vercel gives you |
| `PLAY_SLUG` | `chalkback` |

Run this once for each new value:

```bash
openssl rand -hex 32
```

Don't add `DIRECT_URL` or `NEXT_PUBLIC_DEV_COUNTRY`, because the rules say they don't belong on Vercel. Don't paste any of these values in chat.

**After that:** the next merge to `main` deploys automatically, so CD is done with no custom pipeline. Tell me when the variables are in, and I'll check the deployment, read a real video through the live API, and check that class questions are faster there.

**Three things to know:**
- **CI and deploys:** your CI runs on GitHub as before, but Vercel doesn't wait for it, so a merge deploys straight away. That's another reason to merge only on green.
- **Migrations:** `Deploy migrations` is still off, so database changes won't apply on their own. It needs `PRODUCTION_DATABASE_URL`, the `resolve-baseline` run, and the `PRISMA_BASELINE_RESOLVED` variable. I can do that with you as a separate step.
- **GitHub account:** Vercel is under `shayankhanx1x` and the repo under `devv-shayan`, and the connection worked. If Vercel's GitHub app ever loses access to that repo, deploys will stop.

I also undid a line `vercel link` added to `.gitignore`, and left the blocked `.env.local` check alone. That link step said it created a `.env.local` in the repo root, which only holds Vercel's own local settings. It isn't tracked by git, and you can delete it.

- **Nothing has deployed yet:** connecting the repo doesn't build `main` by itself, and Vercel builds on the next push. The repo rules also say a manual production deploy needs your explicit OK for that one deploy.
- **Preview uses your real database:** there's no separate staging database, so anything done on a preview deploy writes to the same hosted Supabase. That's fine for a hackathon, but keep it in mind.
- **Generated secrets:** `DASHBOARD_API_KEY` has to match your stats dashboard's connection, and `REVENUECAT_WEBHOOK_SECRET` has to match RevenueCat if you use it. They're only in Vercel, so if you need them elsewhere, generate new ones and set both sides.
- **Not set:** `PLAY_INGEST_KEY` and `PLATFORM_BASE_URL`, so no platform marketing emails, and PostHog on the web, so no web PostHog analytics. None of these block a deploy.
- **Site URL:** `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` assumes `chalkback.vercel.app`. Vercel may assign a different address, and I'll correct it if so.

After the first deploy, I'll read a real video through the live API, which is also the first real test of the Gemini-first flow on Vercel.

The first production deploy failed, but the fix is ready in PR #13 and its preview build works. Nothing is live in production yet: both production attempts ended in errors, and the production address returns 404.

**What went wrong:** `apps/web` used `@app/contract` without declaring it. Your local install links every workspace, which hid the problem, but Vercel only installs what's declared. The fix is one line in `apps/web/package.json`, plus the lockfile.

**On the preview build:**
- The build finished as `READY`.
- `GET /api/app/v1/config` returned a real response.
- `POST /api/app/v1/videos` without a login returned 401, as it should.

Merging deploys production automatically. GitHub's typecheck/test/build and migrations checks are still running, so it shouldn't merge until they pass.

**Things to know:**
- **Duplicate attempts:** my retry started a second production deployment, because the first request had gone through. Both failed the same way, so no harm done.
- **Preview login:** preview deploys sit behind Vercel's login, so public phone or browser testing needs the production address.
- **Production address:** `chalkback.vercel.app` isn't assigned yet, and Vercel's alias list doesn't include it. If it isn't assigned on the first successful deploy, I'll claim it or correct `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL`.
- **Node version:** the project setting says Node 26.x, though the rules say the `engines` range in `apps/web/package.json` decides. I'll confirm it runs on Node 22 from the production build.

Once production is up, I still need a signed-in user to test a real video read on the live API. I'll do that through the app, or with a temporary test user that I delete afterward.

The whole app works on production, and it's dramatically faster. I ran the full flow on [chalkback-app.vercel.app](https://chalkback-app.vercel.app) with your account: a new video, a full 4-question class, the help panel and a follow-up.

| Step | Local Mac (hosted DB) | Production |
|---|---|---|
| Next question after an answer | 10–12 seconds | **0.7–2.1 seconds** |
| Starting a class (2 questions) | About 10 seconds | **2.1 seconds** |
| Help panel | 3.6 seconds or more | **1.6 seconds** |
| Reading a new 15-minute video | Not measured | 25.4 seconds |

Moving to Dublin worked: the slowness was the distance to the database.

**Confirmed working on production:**
- **Gemini-first reading:** the logs show Gemini read the video, and Supadata wasn't called, so no credits were used. It found 7 key concepts with real quotes.
- **Maya and Omar:** both raised hands and asked questions. Maya stayed within the explanation.
- **Help panel:** showed a real 7:20 timestamp, and that turn saved as `needed_help`.
- **Follow-up, finally seen on screen:** after my vague answer, Omar said "That still does not explain how getting fired actually caused those new companies to happen."
- **Reaching question 4:** the class ended with "Class finished. You answered 4 questions."

**Problems found:**
1. **Repeated questions:** Omar's question was written at the start, before I answered Maya, so it came out nearly the same as hers: "How can getting fired actually lead to starting successful companies…". That's a side effect of keeping hands up, and it's the most noticeable flaw left in the class.
2. **CORS error:** `/api/lifecycle/session` is blocked for the web app, because I only allowed `/api/app/v1`. It didn't stop anything in this run, but that call won't work from the web app until it's allowed. The fix is a small addition.
3. **Maya's wording:** she invented "when nobody wants to buy what you are making", which isn't something you taught. It's a mild example, and a real student might wonder the same thing.

I marked 8X-36 Done, because Supadata is live and the first deploy is checked. The comment notes that the fallback hasn't yet fired in the live app, and that the video length check is still separate work. There's no record of it in Linear as its own issue yet. This test added another class on your hosted account, and the session's work is on `main`.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=151 session=1f02878a-1847-414e-b657-d91660105c2b]
timestamp: 2026-10-31T13:17:20.675Z
time: 2026-10-31 13:17
chars: 2529

Both fixes work, and every local check passes, including the production build. They're uncommitted on `devvshayan/class-rethink-questions`.

**1. Lifecycle error**
The app can now reach `/api/lifecycle/session` from the browser. This turned out to matter more than the error suggested, because that call records signups, last-seen time and region, which your welcome and win-back emails and per-market numbers rely on. Locally the call now returns 200, but the production rule only takes effect once this deploys.

**2. Repeated questions**
- **At the start:** Maya writes her question first, and Omar is told to avoid it. Maya is never shown Omar's waiting question, so his video details can't leak to her that way.
- **Omar's job:** he now asks about an important idea from the video that you got wrong or left out. In the real class he asked about remembering death, which my explanation never mentioned, instead of copying Maya.
- **After each answer:** a yes/no check asks whether your answer already covered the waiting question. If not, the question stays word for word. If so, the student writes a new one. That check got 4 out of 4 test cases right, and in the real class it replaced Omar's question after I covered it.

My first two attempts failed. Omar copied Maya word for word, then a "keep it or change it" prompt changed the wrong questions. Both are gone.

**Costs and limits:**
- **Slower start:** the students now write one after another, so starting a class takes about a second longer, around 3 seconds on production by my estimate.
- **Extra AI calls:** each answer now adds the yes/no check, and a covered question adds another call. I estimate 3–5 seconds on production in that case, up from 0.7–2.1.
- **Remaining overlap:** Maya can't see Omar's waiting question, so she could still ask something close to it.
- **Questions can change:** if you peek at a waiting question and then answer the other student, it may change. A student rethinking is realistic, so I kept it.
- **Replacement wording:** Omar's new question opened with a reaction, "That explanation about stripping away fear…", and added "market demand", which the video may not cover.

**Test class left open:** clicking "End class" did nothing, because the API was still restarting after the build. That class is saved on your hosted account for the Steve Jobs video, so opening that video will resume it instead of showing the explanation step. Just press "End class" there. The local API is running again in [Web API 3001](#terminal/c13).

I'd do all three of my picks, the first option under each. They're small changes to the same class code, so they fit in the PR that's waiting.

### 1. Maya overlapping Omar's waiting question
- **A. Widen the yes/no check (my pick):** it already asks "did the teacher cover this?". It would also ask "does this overlap what another student just asked?". If it does, Omar writes a new question. This adds no AI call, and Maya still never sees his question.
- **B. Have Omar always write after Maya:** this is simple and needs no check, but Omar would change questions far more often, for no reason.
- **C. Leave it:** two students sometimes ask similar things in real classes. It's the cheapest option, but it's the flaw you noticed.

### 2. A question changing after you've read it
- **A. Show that it changed (my pick):** a small line like "Omar changed his question" appears when it does. It fits the classroom feel, because he's reacting to what you said. It needs a small screen change and no database change.
- **B. Lock a question once you've picked it:** this is the most predictable, but it needs a new column and a new request, and a locked question can go stale, which is what we just fixed.
- **C. Leave it silent:** there's no work, but it can look like a glitch, especially on camera.

### 3. Omar's new question reacting and adding outside ideas
- **A. Tighten the prompt (my pick):** add two rules: replacement questions don't comment on your answer, and Omar only asks about what the video actually says. This takes minutes, but it reduces the problem without guaranteeing it's gone.
- **B. Make Omar quote the video:** he'd give a short quote with each question, and the server would check that it's in the transcript, as key concepts already do. This is the strongest fix, since it enforces "the video is the source of truth", but it adds work and possible retries.
- **C. Show the source:** add "from 7:20" on Omar's questions. It would look great and prove he's using the video, but it depends on B.

I'd do B and C after scoring lands, since a question tied to a timestamp fits gaps and the assessment screen well. For now, Option A gets you most of the way.

Want me to make the three picks now and add them to the waiting PR?

All three are done, and the changed-question behaviour works in a real class. This time the test class ended cleanly, with no question left open.

**The real class run:**
- Omar's waiting question was about how dropping out led to the calligraphy class.
- I answered Maya in a way that also covered that.
- Omar's question then became: "Oh, that answers what I had in mind. How does looking in the mirror every morning asking if today were your last day actually help you make big life choices?"
- That new question comes from the video, and Maya followed up correctly because my answer dodged hers.

**What changed:**
1. **Overlap:** the check now returns three results: open, answered, or overlap. On overlap, Omar quietly writes a new question, and he doesn't claim you answered it. Maya never sees his question.
2. **Changed questions:** when your answer covered a student's question, they say so in their own voice and move on. There's no separate notice on screen.
3. **Staying with the video:** Omar now only asks about what the video says. In 3 test questions and the real class, he stuck to it.

**How accurate it is:**
- **The check:** it got 5 of 6 test cases right. The miss was two calligraphy questions asking slightly different things, which is a borderline call. It's a first version on 6 test cases, so expect the odd mistake.
- **Same opening line:** Omar copied my example wording, "Oh, that answers what I had in mind.", each time. That's fine once in a class, but it would sound repetitive if it came up twice.
- **Maya can still overlap in one direction:** she can't see Omar's question, so overlap only gets fixed when Omar is the one still waiting.

**Gemini limits:** during testing I hit a 429 from Gemini, "exceeded your current quota". It cleared within minutes, so it looks like a per-minute limit on your plan, not a daily one. Each answer can now use up to three AI calls, so a fast class is more likely to hit it. If it happens right after an answer, the rethink step is skipped harmlessly, but a failure in the follow-up or new question can still cause trouble. Run a test class right before you record the demo.

All checks and the production build pass. Everything is uncommitted on `devvshayan/class-rethink-questions`, along with the lifecycle and back-button fixes, and none of it is live until it's merged. Production speed with these extra calls still needs measuring after that. The local API is running in [Web API 3001](#terminal/c14).

You can demo the core loop today: paste a video, teach Maya and Omar, and see your score. The part where weak spots come back later as warm-ups can't be shown yet.

**Ready to demo** (on https://chalkback-app.vercel.app):
1. **Landing page:** Chalkback's own content, with the template material gone.
2. **Add a video:** paste a YouTube link, and it reads the video and shows "Ready to teach" with the key ideas hidden.
3. **Explain:** teach the video back in your own words.
4. **Teach the class:** Maya only knows what you tell her and asks about what confuses her. Omar has seen the video and checks you against it. You choose whose question to answer.
5. **Result:** your score, such as "3 of 7" in our live test, with each key idea marked right, wrong or missed.

Scoring failures no longer stop a class. Tests cover this, but it's not something you'd show in a demo.

**Not ready to demo:**
- **Gaps and warm-ups:** the screens and scheduling are merged, but nothing saves gaps yet, so warm-ups always show "Nothing due". This is Arif's 8X-17, and it's the biggest missing piece because it's the "learning that sticks" part of the theme.
- **Video links and "Not in the video":** both are part of Arif's 8X-16, along with student reactions and skipped questions.
- **Voice:** paused, so the app is typing only.

**Things to avoid while recording:**
- **Short windows:** the tab bar partly covers "Teach this to the class", so use a tall window or your phone.
- **Web first load:** it can come up blank, so open the app before you start recording.
- **End class:** ending early removes unanswered questions, so answer them all first.
- **Scoring:** it can be generous. One test marked "Dropping Out" correct when it was never mentioned, so explain the video properly and leave one idea out on purpose. That way the result shows a clear miss.

For the 8X-33 Loom, record steps 2–5 now on one short video, and you can stay under a minute by trimming the wait while it reads the video. If 8X-17 lands before Feedback 2 tomorrow, add warm-ups as a second Loom.

The scoring bug is fixed on `devvshayan/fix-explanation-scoring`, but it isn't committed or live yet, so the live app can still give scores like 0 of 5.

**Cause:** Gemini was scoring correctly, but it returned each idea's title with its quote attached. Our code expects the exact title, so nothing matched and every idea was marked Missed. Results that were marked correct or wrong were thrown away.

**Fixes:**
- **Exact titles only:** Gemini now has to pick from the video's exact titles. See `scoreResponseJsonSchema` in [score-explanation.ts](apps/web/src/server/classroom/score-explanation.ts).
- **No more false zeros:** if a reply matches none of the ideas, it now counts as a failed scoring instead of a score of 0. The class still runs, and scoring is tried again when it ends.
- **Tests:** I added two, one for that failure and one for the exact titles.

**Checks:**

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| The exact prompt from your Matt Cutts class, run 4 times against real Gemini | **2 of 5 every time**, with the backwards claim marked **Wrong** |
| Web tests | 214 of 214 passed |
| Typecheck, lint and format | Passed |

I haven't run a full class through the app with this fix, or the full `local-review` gates yet. I'll run those gates before pushing.

**Still true after the fix:**
- **Old classes:** they keep their old scores, including the 0 of 5.
- **Scoring only looks at your first explanation:** what you say to Maya and Omar during class doesn't change it.
- **Generous scoring:** this fix doesn't address that, such as "Dropping Out" being marked correct when it was never mentioned. The Panic Monster mistake never showed as Wrong, but this run doesn't tell us whether that was the title bug. The Steve Jobs run scored 3 of 7 with one Wrong, so that class of scoring may still be too generous.

Right: the score ignores the class session and only looks at your first explanation. That's how the product brief describes it, so it isn't a bug. "Score an explanation" takes just the explanation, the key ideas and the transcript.

| | Based on | Affects the score? |
|---|---|---|
| **Score (2 of 5)** | Your first explanation, scored before the class starts | Yes |
| **Student questions** | The score picks what Omar asks about | No, it's the other way round |
| **Your answers in class** | Used by the students to react and follow up | No |
| **"How each question went"** | Answered, Needed help or Skipped | No, it's shown separately |

So in your demo, you told Maya "I think I said it backwards" and corrected the point, but Small Sustainable Changes would still show as Wrong.

**I'd keep it this way.** The score measures what you could explain on your own, and class is where you get to fix it. Fixing something right after a student points it out doesn't prove you'll remember it, which is why missed ideas come back as warm-ups days later. Answering a warm-up correctly is what closes a gap, so that's where your progress should show. That also fits "learning that sticks".

The one catch is that a user who corrects themselves may feel the Wrong mark is unfair. A small wording fix would help: label the score something like "From your first explanation". Viewers would then see why it doesn't change during class. Arif's gaps work won't need to change either way, because every missed or wrong idea becomes a gap.

Changing it so class answers raise the score would need a new AI step, and I wouldn't do that before Feedback 2.

No, keep gaps hidden until the class ends. The score is worked out before class, but only so the students know what to ask about. That's already how the app works: "Your score stays hidden until the class ends", and the server doesn't send the score to the app until then.

Showing gaps first would break the main idea:
- **It gives away the answers:** if you see "you missed Growing Self-Confidence" before class, you can just go and look it up. Maya and Omar's questions would then have nothing left to test.
- **The questions are how you find the gaps:** being asked something you can't answer is what makes it stick. Reading a list of mistakes doesn't do that.
- **It would change your answers:** you'd be answering to fix a score, not teaching.

So the order stays:
1. **Explain:** the score is calculated quietly.
2. **Class:** the students go after your weak spots without naming them.
3. **Results:** you see your score, your gaps and where they are in the video.
4. **Days later:** each gap comes back as a warm-up.

Your demo showed this working: Maya caught the backwards claim through a question, and nothing told you it was wrong until the results screen. If you want to explain it in a Loom, you could say: "The app knows your gaps from the start, but the students make you find them yourself."

Yes, they'd think exactly that, and they'd be right to. I said a wording change would cover it, but it won't: if you fix something in class and still see Wrong, the app looks like it ignored you. It also makes teaching the class feel pointless, and that's the main thing we're asking people to do.

The first score is still worth keeping, because it shows what you knew on your own. The fix is to show **both**:

> **2 of 5** in your first explanation
> **4 of 5** by the end of class

| Idea | Result |
|---|---|
| The 30-Day Challenge Concept | Correct from the start |
| Small Sustainable Changes | Wrong at first, **fixed in class** |
| Writing a Novel in a Month | Still missed |

- **Your effort counts:** you can see the score go up because you taught the class, and that's a nice moment to show in a demo.
- **Spaced repetition still works:** an idea you fixed in class still comes back as a warm-up, with a note like "You fixed this in class. It'll come back in a couple of days to check it stuck." Only a correct warm-up closes the gap.
- **Arif's gaps work doesn't change:** gaps still come from the first score, so 8X-17 isn't affected.

**What it takes:**
- One extra AI call when the class ends, scoring your explanation plus your class answers.
- One new field in the class results, which older app versions can safely ignore.
- Changes to the results screen, plus registering the events first, since it's a user-visible change.

It's a small job, but it touches the Assessment screen Arif built, so tell him before I start. Generous scoring could also mark an idea as fixed when it wasn't, so I'd test it on your Matt Cutts class: your "I said it backwards" answer should count as fixed, and a vague answer shouldn't.

I'd merge the scoring fix first, because the live app can still show wrong scores until then.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=170 session=1f02878a-1847-414e-b657-d91660105c2b]
timestamp: 2026-11-01T15:57:21.102Z
time: 2026-11-01 15:57
chars: 894

Show both scores after the class, on the results screen. Nothing changes before class: the first score stays hidden, as it does now.

Showing the first score before class would have the same problem as showing gaps early. "2 of 5" tells you that you got things wrong, so you'd go looking for what's missing and treat Maya and Omar's questions as a test. The class only works if you find your gaps by being asked.

The order would be:
1. **Explain:** the first score is calculated quietly.
2. **Class:** no score is shown, and the students ask about your weak spots.
3. **End of class:** the app scores your explanation plus your class answers.
4. **Results:** you see both scores together: "2 of 5 in your first explanation, 4 of 5 by the end of class".

Seeing them side by side is also the best moment on that screen: you find out where you started and how much the class helped, all at once.

Both tickets are created, the two-score results are built, and they work in the browser. It's on your local build only, so it isn't committed or live yet.

**Tickets:**
- [8X-40](https://linear.app/8x-hackathon/issue/8X-40/score-reply-matched-no-ideas-so-every-idea-showed-missed): the 0 of 5 scoring bug
- [8X-41](https://linear.app/8x-hackathon/issue/8X-41/show-the-first-score-and-the-end-of-class-score): the first score and end-of-class score

**Browser tests** (same Matt Cutts explanation, real account and real Gemini):

| Class | First explanation | End of class | What the screen showed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Clear correction: "I said it backwards" | 2 of 5 | **3 of 5** | Small Sustainable Changes under "Fixed in class", labelled "Wrong at first" |
| Only a vague "I'm not sure" answer | 2 of 5 | 2 of 5 | No "Fixed in class" section, and everything else unchanged |

**A bug I caught while testing:** in the first run, my "I'm not sure" answer turned Writing a Novel from Missed into Wrong. The class can now only raise a result, so being unsure never makes an idea worse. That result is the one rule I changed from what we discussed, where I'd only said correct ideas stay correct.

**What changed:**
- **Scoring:** one extra AI call when a class ends, if you answered at least one question. It took about a second in testing.
- **Storage:** no migration needed, because the new result is saved with the class's existing scores.
- **If the extra call fails:** the screen shows the first score on its own. Tests cover this, but I didn't trigger it in the browser.
- **Events:** `assessment_viewed` now also sends `final_correct_count` and `fixed_count`.

**Checks:** web tests 221 of 221, mobile tests 238 of 238, and contract tests 13 of 13 all pass, along with typecheck, lint, format and the store rules. The events check passed before my last small fix, which didn't touch events. I'll run the full `local-review` gates before pushing.

**Things to know:**
- **Warm-up promise:** "Fixed in class" says these ideas will come back in a warm-up, but that won't happen until Arif finishes 8X-17. The home screen already makes the same promise.
- **Tell Arif:** this changes his Assessment screen (8X-16). Gaps should still come from the first score, `result`, and not `result_after_class`.
- **Maya's reaction:** after my vague answer she said "That makes sense now", which is too generous. This was already happening, and I didn't change it.
- **Your account:** testing added three more ended classes.

Yes, that's fine for now. Only 2 accounts exist, yours and most likely Arif's, so nobody else is affected and the seed data is doing no harm. I'd change two things.

**1. Delete only the seed rows, not the whole database.** A few rows are easy to remove: the 2 `seed-due-gap` topics, their 2 classes and their 2 gaps. A full flush is riskier:
- It would wipe the real classes you've been running, including your Matt Cutts and Steve Jobs history.
- If `profiles` gets cleared, your logins still exist in Supabase Auth but have no matching profile, which can break sign-in.
- It would wipe `analytics_events`, which your stats and retention numbers come from.
- If `_prisma_migrations` gets cleared, the database will look like it has no migrations applied.

**2. Clean up before anyone else sees it, not at the very end.** "After development" is too late, because Feedback 2 is tomorrow:
- **Before you record a Loom:** a Photosynthesis warm-up could show on your home screen. That could even be useful for showing warm-ups, as long as you say it's seeded test data.
- **Once 8X-17 works:** delete the seed rows, because real gaps will replace them.
- **Before Nov 6, or if a reviewer gets the link:** do a final check for test rows, since live users share this database.

Tell Arif to run the seed only for testing and to remove the rows when he's done. That's enough for the hackathon, so you don't need a separate database. If Chalkback continues after Nov 6, set up separate development and production databases then.

This kit takes its look from the Infisical page and applies it to Chalkback's screens.

**What I took from Infisical:**
- **Highlights:** white background and near-black text, with lime highlighting the important words. I've used it on "learn", the gaps you still have to learn, and "actually stuck" in the tagline.
- **Labels:** small, all-caps labels in a monospace font, such as HOME, TEACH, 2 / 4 and RESULTS. This replaces the template's dot-and-eyebrow style.
- **Buttons and cards:** black buttons with an arrow, thin hairline borders and slightly rounded corners. Rounded bubbles are left out.
- **Pixel blocks:** used in the logo mark and as a small detail at the bottom of the screen.
- **Gradient:** the lime-to-orange-to-pink gradient is used only for the score card, so getting your result feels like a reward.
- **Fonts:** Geist for text and Geist Mono for labels.

**How it fits the app:**
- **Students:** Maya is orange and Omar is violet, both taken from the gradient, so you can see who is speaking.
- **Light and dark:** it's light by default, and a dark version follows the same idea with black backgrounds and lime highlights, like Infisical's black sections.
- **Release:** it can all go out as an OTA update, except the splash background colour, which needs the next store build.

**Before I build:**

I've dropped the wireframes and redesigned the four main screens from scratch, in the style you liked.

**What changes from the current app:**
- **Steps instead of tabs:** the Explain, Teach and Gaps tabs become a 3-step progress bar at the top: Explain, Teach, Results. You can't skip ahead, which suits how the class works.
- **Home:** a due warm-up appears at the top as a lime card. Pasting a link takes one line, with the button inside the field. Your topics show a progress bar of known, closing and still-missing ideas.
- **Explain:** a full-screen writing area with a word count, so it feels like writing rather than filling in a form.
- **Class:** works like a chat. Maya and Omar sit at the top and the raised hand shows on whoever is waiting. The answer box is fixed at the bottom, with Check video, Skip and End as small buttons above it. This also fixes the tab bar covering the button.
- **Results:** one card shows the score going from 2/5 to 3/5, followed by a line from a student. Ideas still to learn come first, then ones fixed in class, each with a play button for that moment in the video.

**What needs data the app doesn't have yet:**
- **Topic progress bars and Continue:** need a topic list, which is part of 8X-13 (Arif). Until that exists, home shows only the warm-up and link box.
- **Student line on results:** needs reactions, which are part of 8X-16 (Arif).
- **Play buttons on results:** need the YouTube id added to the class data. It's a small change and I can do it.
- **Warm-up card:** needs gaps, which are part of 8X-17 (Arif). Until then it only shows seeded test data.

I'll build the look and the layouts now, and leave space for the parts that need Arif's data. That way his work fits in without either of us redoing the layout. Tell Arif before I start, because we'll be editing the same files.

|---|---|

| Centred intro in a framed box | "One study loop for everything you watch" |
| Three product sections, each with its own highlight colour, a feature list on the left and a UI on a grainy gradient on the right | Explain on lime and green, Teach the class on yellow and gold, See what stuck on peach and red |
| Pixel steps into a black section with a two-tone headline and a flow diagram | "You forget most of a video in a week. Maya makes sure you don't." The diagram shows gap found → 2 days → warm-up → closed. |
| "10 billion every day" with a dot-matrix graphic and 4 feature cells | "Everyone forgets. Teaching it makes it stick." with a dot field turning lime, plus 4 cells |
| Customer story cards on grainy photos | Maya and Omar cards with one of their lines each. I didn't add fake reviews. |
| Lime call-to-action panel | "The fastest way to learn it is to teach it." |
| Footer with mono column headings | Same |

**Details I matched:**

- **Highlight colour:** `#f7fe62`, Infisical's own.
- **Lines:** thin borders around every grid.

- **Customer logos and tweet wall:** these need real users we don't have yet.

The section backgrounds in the mock above now move.

**What moves:**
- **Section gradients:** each one drifts slowly. The product panels, the black section, the Maya and Omar cards and the lime call-to-action panel each loop every 9 to 14 seconds, so they don't all move together.
- **Grain:** the grain on each gradient flickers slightly, like film. Infisical's grainy backgrounds do the same.
- **Hero grid:** the dotted grid behind the stacked windows pans slowly.
- **Dot field:** a lime band moves across the grey dots in "Why teaching works".

**How it'll work on the real site:**
- **Built with CSS only:** no JavaScript, so it costs almost nothing to run and doesn't slow the page.
- **Reduced motion:** people with "reduce motion" turned on in their phone or computer settings see still gradients, which is an accessibility standard.
- **Offscreen sections:** if a phone struggles with them, I'll pause animations on sections that aren't on screen.

[CLAUDE_LOG_ENTRY type=RESPONSE num=190 session=1f02878a-1847-414e-b657-d91660105c2b]
timestamp: 2026-11-01T19:57:34.762Z
time: 2026-11-01 19:57
chars: 1999

These backgrounds are generated by code as the page runs, not drawn as images. Each one comes from the product itself, so another learning app couldn't simply copy it.

| Section | What's running |
|---|---|
| **Explain** | The real transcript of the Matt Cutts video, set in a mono font and moving with a noise field. Its key ideas ("30 days", "memorable", "self confidence") light up lime one by one. These are the same ideas the scorer checks. |
| **Teach the class** | Moving noise shaded in gold, built from hard pixels arranged in a fixed pattern. It looks like printed grain, never repeats and has no image file. |
| **See what stuck** | A red-orange pixel field with rings spreading out from points, like gaps coming back as warm-ups. Each ring is brighter than the last, like memory getting stronger with each warm-up. |
| **Black section** | 900 bits of chalk dust drift with a current and leave trails. Every so often a band of them turns lime, like an idea being remembered. |
| **Call to action** | A coarse lime pixel field whose edges break into moving blocks, like Infisical's pixel steps. |

**Why they're hard to copy:**
- **Built in code:** the noise and pixel effects are written by hand rather than taken from a library or an image, so someone would have to rebuild them.
- **Tied to our data:** the transcript background uses the actual video's words and key ideas, so it only works for a product that already has that data.
- **Meaning:** the ripples and chalk dust match the warm-up idea, so they make sense on our page in a way they wouldn't elsewhere.

**Performance and accessibility on the live site:**
- **Lightweight:** each one is drawn at low resolution and enlarged, at about 20 to 30 frames a second.
- **Pausing:** each pauses when it's scrolled out of view.
- **Reduced motion:** visitors with "reduce motion" turned on see one still frame.
- **Transcript content:** on the real site it can rotate through sample videos, or show the video the visitor just pasted.

**1B, voice waves,** is now the Explain background. I've drawn six new options for the black section. Each one has the headline on top, so you can check the text stays readable over it.

- **4D, tunnel:** rings pull you towards the centre, so it feels like going deeper into a topic.
- **4E, memory map:** moving contour lines, like a changing map of what you know. It's subtle, and the headline stays very readable.
- **4F, cells:** living cells whose edges pulse lime, like ideas linking up.
- **4G, lime horizon:** a striped sun over a moving grid. It's the boldest and most retro, but it might be too much for this section.
- **4H, neurons:** lime signals travel between points, like your brain recalling something. It fits a learning app best.
- **4I, pixel tide:** big pixel blocks rise and fall. It's the closest to Infisical's pixel steps.

**My pick is 4H, neurons:** it matches the line "Maya makes sure you don't", and it's busy at the edges but calm behind the text. 4E is my second choice if you want something quieter.

4D, the tunnel, is now the black section's background. That completes the set:

| Section | Background |
|---|---|
| Explain | 1B · Voice waves, green |
| Teach the class | 2 · Gold pixel noise |
| See what stuck | 3 · Warm-up ripples, red-orange |
| Black section | 4D · Tunnel, lime on black |

The hero keeps the slowly moving dotted grid behind the stacked app windows. It's quieter than the others, so the headline stays the main focus.

**What building it involves:**
- **Branch:** a new branch from `main`, with changes to the website in `apps/web` only. The app's screens follow in a separate change.
- **Backgrounds:** one shared, tested background component, with each section supplying its own pattern. Each pauses when off screen and shows a still frame for people with reduced motion turned on.
- **The rest of the page:** Inter Tight and JetBrains Mono fonts, the lime highlight, square buttons, thin lines and section labels, all set in the brand settings so the app can reuse them.
- **Checks:** I'll check it in the browser at desktop and phone width and run all the local checks, then open a PR. The site only changes once the PR is merged.

The full page above now uses every background you picked.

**Changes in this version:**
- **Maya and Omar cards:** each now has its own pixel background, in orange and violet, so they match the rest of the page. The earlier version still used plain CSS gradients for these.
- **Tunnel behind the black section:** I moved its centre to the right and dimmed it on the left, so the headline, timeline and three features stay readable.
- **Text over busy backgrounds:** labels and buttons over moving backgrounds sit on small white or dark boxes. This keeps text readable in every frame, the way Infisical places its UI cards on top.
- **"Why teaching works":** I removed the dot-field strip. Its pattern clashed with the others, and the headline and four feature cells stand well on their own.

The background code passed a syntax check and a dry run before I drew the page, so the blank-box problem shouldn't happen again. I can't see the result myself, so tell me if any section looks empty or wrong.

Here are four hero options. Each runs its own code rather than a video or image, so copying one means rebuilding it.

- **A, live class:** a 15-second class plays on loop. Maya raises her hand and types a question, you answer, Omar points out what you skipped, the score goes from 2/5 to 3/5, and the missed idea moves into a warm-up. It explains the whole product without anyone scrolling.
- **B, pixel students:** Maya and Omar are drawn as pixel characters, made from shapes defined in code, in the same pixel style as the section backgrounds. They take turns raising a lime hand with a speech bubble, and hovering over one calls on them. It's the most distinctive option and gives the students a face.
- **C, forgetting headline:** "Teach it back." is made of about 2,000 particles. Every 10 seconds it falls apart into dust, labelled "forgetting…", and pulls back together, labelled "warm-up: remembering…", and the lime highlights return. Your cursor pushes the particles out of the way. It acts out the main idea instead of only describing it.
- **D, lens and depth:** the app windows sit at different depths and tilt towards your cursor, while a lens moving through the dot grid enlarges dots and turns them lime. It's the most polished, but also the closest to what other sites do.

**My pick is A with C's idea in it:** the live class takes the right side, and the headline breaks apart and rebuilds once on page load. Visitors see how it works and the "learning that sticks" idea in the first 15 seconds. If you want only one, take **A**: it's the best for judges seeing the product for the first time.

On phones and for people with reduced motion turned on, every option shows a finished still frame. For A, that's the completed class with the 3/5 result.

I built the landing page with B, the pixel students, as the hero. It works locally at desktop and phone width, and all 11 local checks pass. It's on the branch `devvshayan/landing-redesign`, uncommitted, so the live site hasn't changed. You can see it now at `localhost:3000` in the Browser pane.

**What's on the page:**
- **Hero:** Maya and Omar are drawn live in pixels. Maya has hair in a bun, Omar wears glasses, and they both blink. They take turns raising a lime hand with their question. Pointing at or tapping one calls on them, and their hand goes up on the side away from their speech bubble.
- **Sections:** How it works, then Explain (voice waves), Teach the class (gold), See what stuck (ripples), the black band (lime tunnel) with Infisical-style pixel steps above it, Why teaching works, Meet the class (Maya and Omar cards), Questions, and the lime closing band.
- **Nav and footer:** redone in the new style, with a new pixel logo: a 3x3 grid with one lime square.

**How it's built:**
- **Backgrounds:** a shared engine in `apps/web/src/components/landing/pixel/` generates the noise and draws it in pixels. Each background pauses when off screen and shows a still frame for reduced motion.
- **Tests:** 9 new tests cover the engine. They check that the same seed always draws the same output, that each background uses only its own colours, and that a raised hand turns lime.
- **Events:** I registered the new button placements (`hero`, `loop`, `final`) before wiring them. The "How it works" section now fires the existing event for getting past the first screen.
- **Unused files:** five old landing files that were no longer used are deleted.

**Things you should know:**
- **The whole website is now light:** Support, Privacy, Terms and Pricing use the light version of the brand colours. They read fine, but the support form still has the old rounded blue button.
- **Website colours:** they're defined in one website-only file, `landing.css`, and not in `packages/brand` as the repo convention asks. That's because the app doesn't use them yet. If the app adopts this look, they should move to the brand package.
- **Font:** I used Inter Tight because Infisical's headline font is paid.
- **Tab icon:** the browser tab icon (`app/icon.tsx`) is still the old letter "C", not the new pixel logo.
- **Database checks:** I couldn't run them locally because Postgres isn't installed. CI will run them, and this change doesn't touch the database.
